I. 
Overall assessment of the Secretariat 
1. 
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks: 
Strengths 
Points of caution 
Significant efforts made in revising financing 
structure for GCF funding, from pure grants 
request to a mix of grants and concessional loans 
Mobilization of the equity contribution by 
beneficiaries under the matching grant fund 
should be monitored and reported. 
As an adaptation project, incrementality 
reasoning is provided in a qualitative manner, 
with GCF funding to be used for resilient 
irrigation systems, tropical greenhouses, 
drainage, and climate-proofing of infrastructure; 
while co-financing from the IFAD will support an 
increase in agricultural productivity 
 
Proven on-granting mechanisms, which have been 
tested with previously implemented IFAD 
projects; these will meet the needs of the 
communities while strengthening ownership of 
producer organizations 
 
2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled “List of proposed 
conditions and recommendations”.  
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment 
2.1 
Project background 
3. 
The project aims to increase resilience of smallholder farmers in Belize to climate 
change impacts, mainly increased droughts, flooding, and significant rainfall pattern variations 
between seasons. These impacts have negative consequences on agricultural yields of important 
commodities for the country. In response to these climate impacts, the project will develop and 
climate-proof selected value chains of smallholder farmers, which strengthen economic stability 
and resilience. 
4. 
The project will also increase access to markets through rehabilitation of critical 
infrastructure. It will deploy grant award and on-lending mechanisms and work with producers’ 
organizations and local communities to meet their needs in addressing the impacts of climate 
change. Through this project, around 30,000 people in 23 communities in five districts will

=== PAGE 95 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 95 
 
 
benefit directly from increased access to climate information services for agricultural planning, 
climate-resilient agricultural practices and greater access to markets through resilient 
rehabilitated roads. 
5. 
The project is structured in three components: 
(a) 
Climate resilient value chains development (CRVC);  
(b) 
Climate resilient rural infrastructure and assets development (CRRIA); and 
(c) 
Cross-cutting activities and project management 
6. 
The project requests USD 6 million in GCF grants and USD 2 million in the form of a 
concessional loan, which collectively is 40 per cent of the total project cost. Co-financing will be 
provided by:  
(a) 
The Government of Belize, which will cover taxes and costs for the financing of road 
infrastructure and irrigation and drainage works;  
(b) 
The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), which will provide loans 
for investment and technical assistance to improve production, value-added activities 
and improving market access. It will also cover the costs of strengthening organizations 
and public institutions; and  
(c) 
Project participants, which will provide counterpart financing under the Matching Grand 
Fund. 
7. 
Since it was first submitted, the funding proposal has gone through a rigorous review 
process and significant work has been done to strengthen it. The financing structure was 
revised and reallocated from a request purely for grants to one that mixes grants and 
concessional loans, which will support income-generating agricultural activities for adaptation. 
Greater clarity on the grant award and on-lending mechanisms has been provided, with a 
detailed project implementation manual annexed to the funding proposal. 
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis 
Component 1: Climate resilient value chains development; (total cost: USD 7.9 million; GCF cost: USD 
3.5 million, or 44 per cent) 
8. 
Component 1 has three outputs: development of infrastructure and production plans; 
strengthening of producers’ organizations; and development of value chains. GCF funding will 
be used for a climate vulnerability assessment, an analysis of value chains, and a market 
assessment as well as to develop value chains, including through a matching grant fund (MGF).  
9. 
The need for an infrastructure and production plan based on a climate vulnerability 
assessment and market analysis is presented in a clear manner in the project structure. The 
proposal should, however, demonstrate how this plan will inform the implementation of other 
activities in the project, particularly the selection criteria for proposals submitted for the MGF 
and infrastructure funding award, so that development of the plan will have both short-term 
(project-level) and long-term contributions and effects. 
10. 
The proposed interventions of combining climate-resilient agricultural commodities and 
technologies are assessed to be adequate to meet the needs of smallholder farmers in 
developing a value chain and accessing markets. The selected fruit and vegetable commodities 
have been produced in the target areas during the last three years (i.e. their production 
represents a business as usual scenario) and were selected for their high productivity and 
market potential. Upon request by the Secretariat, a climate rationale was provided by the 
accredited entity (AE) for the selection of vegetables, fruits and honey in relation to their 
resilience to climate impacts. The selected commodities are also assessed to be appropriate in

=== PAGE 96 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 96 
 
 
the context of climate impacts, given their stronger resilience to extreme weather events 
compared to tree-based fruits, which were not selected. While the selected commodities are still 
vulnerable to extreme weather events, combined with agronomic technological improvements, 
as presented in the funding proposal, the proposed agricultural interventions are expected to 
bring stable yields and production despite the anticipated impacts of climate change. 
Component 2: Climate resilient rural infrastructure and assets development (total cost: USD 8.5 
million; GCF cost: USD 4.1 million, or 48 per cent) 
11. 
Component 2 aims to improve both agricultural productivity and market access through 
rehabilitation and provision of new roads as well as drainage and irrigation infrastructure. The 
component will increase agricultural production and access to markets. 
12. 
The proposal states that GCF funding will be used to cover any additional costs for 
climate proofing of infrastructure. The budget details show that GCF funding will cover climate-
proofing of rural and ancillary roads (30 per cent of the total cost of roads rehabilitation) and 70 
per cent of small-scale irrigation and drainage infrastructure. During the review process, the AE 
strengthened the climate rationale by adding a description of the additional climate proofing 
activities that would be carried out under for this component, which was deemed satisfactory. 
13. 
The component will be implemented through an infrastructure investment funding 
award, with a GCF contribution of USD 3.9 million. The fund will blend the IFAD loan and GCF 
grants, with the GCF funding dedicated to climate proofing of infrastructure. Given the full 
concessionality requested for the GCF funding, on-granting should be carefully monitored and 
reported to demonstrate that GCF resources are only used for adaptation activities for public 
goods.  
14. 
The component also proposes to create a climate information system to provide 
accurate climate information to farmers to assist their agricultural planning. The project will 
need to report on the uptake of the climate information system by beneficiary farmers and 
demonstrate how such information is integrated into the national system and its interlinkage 
with the adoption of resilient agricultural practices. 
Component 3: Cross-cutting activities and project management (total cost: USD 3.7 million; GCF 
cost: USD 0.3 million, or 8 per cent) 
15. 
There are also cross-cutting activities, including capacity-building for public authorities, 
applied research and development, and policy engagement and consultation. These will have a 
total cost of USD 0.3 million, of which the GCF funding contribution is USD 0.13 million (43 per 
cent). The proposed activities are assessed to be adequate and needed to support components 1 
and 2, as well as to increase awareness and encourage beneficiaries to engage and participate in 
the proposed project. 
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
3.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
 
         Scale: N/A 
16. 
As an adaptation project, the proposal reports an expected total number of direct and 
indirect beneficiaries. Beneficiary selection criteria is mainly driven by consideration of the 
poverty rate and potential for accessing and contributing to market and value chain 
development, with reference to climate change vulnerability. The number of direct beneficiaries 
was estimated using the number of poor households in rural Belize contained in the country’s 
2010 poverty assessment. More precision in making the estimation could have improved its 
accuracy.

=== PAGE 97 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 97 
 
 
17. 
Climate rationale and adaptation needs are elaborated and justified in a reasonable 
manner: Belize is a small island developing State located in the Caribbean hurricane belt that is 
highly vulnerable to seasonal variations and extreme weather events. The proposal provides 
some evidence to show climate change impacts (increased variability of precipitation patterns) 
on decline in yields and losses of agricultural production.  
18. 
The project will combine adaptation measures and economic development through 
grant award and on-lending mechanisms, which will bring high impacts for participating 
beneficiaries. The MGF will include investments in renewable energy installation and efficient 
irrigation, which may have some mitigation potential (if replacing diesel-powered engines). This 
was not reported as a core indicator but should be reported as a co-benefit of the project. 
19. 
The proposal is expected to reach 9.3 per cent of the population in Belize and targets 23 
communities in five districts. Given the size of the country, the scale of the intervention is 
assessed to have substantial climate adaptation benefits.  
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
   
          Scale: N/A 
20. 
Paradigm shift potential is demonstrated through a shift away from farming practices 
that are costly and environmentally unsustainable (due to the use of agrochemicals and 
fertilizers) to a more climate-resilient use of varieties and practices that will enhance the 
resilience of smallholder farmers while increasing incomes from productive agriculture. 
Proposed agronomic techniques are aligned with good practices in achieving adaptation results. 
These solutions have high potential to be scaled up and replicated in other regions of the 
country.  
21. 
The proposal demonstrates good potential for scaling up and replication through using 
grant award and on-lending mechanisms that have been tested and proved through previous 
IFAD projects. During the implementation, the project should demonstrate that the expected 
financial contribution from beneficiary producer organizations has indeed been mobilized to 
implement their projects under the MGF and the infrastructure funding award. This will greatly 
increase ownership of the assets, technologies and infrastructure to be built by the farmers, 
thereby ensuring sustainability of the proposed project. Sustainability is also foreseen to be 
ensured through inclusion in the project of a variety of capacity-building and policy support 
activities.  
22. 
A theory of change is presented in the funding proposal, which proposes solutions to 
support smallholder farmers in value chain development and market access. It shows clearly 
how existing constraints are expected to be addressed for smallholders and producers’ 
organizations in building resilience to climate change. While expected benefits for beneficiaries 
are clear in the outcomes of the project, its adaptation results and impacts will need to be 
monitored during project implementation as they will depend on effective interlinkage between 
activities (e.g. development of a climate information system and production of resilient 
agriculture). 
23. 
The exit strategy relies on a set of soft measures (institutional strengthening and 
knowledge building) as well as an expectation of private sector participation. During the review 
process, the AE was requested to provide more clarity on ownership of assets and financial 
planning for assets and infrastructure. While it is expected that project participants will take 
ownership of assets under the MGF, more concrete financial analysis and plans for the operation 
and maintenance of public infrastructure would be beneficial in the future.

=== PAGE 98 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 98 
 
 
3.3 
Sustainable development potential      
             
          Scale: N/A 
24. 
The proposal demonstrates high potential for sustainable development. Proposed 
interventions are proven solutions that are environmentally sound. The project should ensure 
careful use of pesticides and fertilizers as well as use of sound environmental practices if wells 
are drilled during the implementation to avoid any contamination of aquifers and unsustainable 
extraction of groundwater. 
25. 
The project will generate substantial economic and social benefits for farmers through 
increased agricultural production and income. The project will bring a combination of increased 
income (i.e. poverty reduction) and increased capacity to address climate change challenges. 
Given the good potential for income generation, this will also bring positive social impacts for 
the society. 
26. 
Gender considerations have been taken into account in the project design with an aim to 
mainstream women in agricultural development. 
3.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
                     Scale: N/A 
27. 
The vulnerability of the country and target beneficiaries and their adaptation needs are 
generally explained throughout the proposal. The vulnerability of the target population is 
explained in terms of poverty levels. Given the significant dependence of the country’s economy 
on agriculture, the needs of the beneficiaries are positively acknowledged. 
28. 
Belize is a small island developing State located in the Caribbean hurricane belt that is 
vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The project will deliver public environmental and 
economic goods along with increased incomes for producers’ organizations. It will provide 
financial resources to overcome barriers that cannot be addressed by the market. There are 
currently few private sector investments in resilient agriculture in the target areas, but the 
proposal has good potential to bring in the private sector in the long term. 
29. 
The proposal sufficiently explains and justifies the absence of alternative sources of 
financing. Elevated levels of public debt (100 per cent of gross domestic product) and the fact 
that most beneficiaries will have to turn to credit unions for capital to meet the co-financing 
requirements, justify the need for GCF grants and concessional loans. 
3.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
    
 
          Scale: N/A 
30. 
The proposal shows that the proposed project contributes to the country’s Nationally 
Determined Contribution and the National Agricultural Sector Adaptation Strategy (2015). The 
proposal is part of a broader strategy for the agricultural sector, with many international 
organizations involved. The project demonstrates complementarity with other donors’ projects 
and initiatives to achieve targets in the agriculture sector in line with national climate change 
strategies. 
31. 
The Ministry of Economic Development (MED) has been selected as executing entity due 
to its long and successful partnership with IFAD. However, its track record of managing 
investments of a similar size was not provided. A financial management assessment of MED has 
been undertaken in accordance with a policy of IFAD and has been submitted in an annex to the 
funding proposal. 
32. 
The proposal demonstrates that project design and site selection was done in a 
participatory manner with the relevant ministries and stakeholders, ensuring participation of 
women. The project requires technical support from the Ministry of Agriculture, which will 
require inter-ministry coordination.

=== PAGE 99 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 99 
 
 
3.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
         Scale: N/A 
33. 
The proposal demonstrates adequate cost-effectiveness, with an economic internal rate 
of return (IRR) of 18.8 per cent over 15 years and a financial IRR of 18.8 per cent.  
34. 
The funding proposal demonstrates financial adequacy and minimum concessionality. 
The choice of a mix of grants and a concessional loan is assessed to be adequate considering the 
proposed activities and their corresponding financial instruments. Grants are for the activities 
that have a low and long-term profitability and for which costs cannot be fully recovered by the 
beneficiaries. The concessional loan is for activities under the MGF, which is expected to 
produce incomes for participating producer organizations. 
35. 
There is a mismatch in tenor and grace periods between the IFAD and GCF loans. The AE 
provided two justifications for the mismatch: (a) it was decided by the Government of Belize as 
they are sovereign backed loans; and (b) the higher concessionality of the GCF loan terms 
reflects the high vulnerability of Belize to climate change and limits the country’s short-term 
burden of debt. This justification will be strengthened by targeting GCF loans to climate 
adaptation activities, and this consideration should be reflected in the selection criteria of the 
MGF.  
36. 
Long-term financial viability will be ensured if the project delivers financial returns of 
15 per cent as projected. There should be sufficient capital to operate and maintain the 
investments. 
IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
37. 
E&S risk category: The AE has classified the project as having moderate environmental and 
social risks and potential impacts equivalent to GCF’s category B risk categorization. The AE’s 
risk categorization has considered potential medium-level adverse environmental and social 
risks and impacts related to improvement of rural roads, construction of community based 
agro-processing facilities, use of natural resources such as surface and groundwater, and 
development of small-scale irrigation and drainage systems.  The AE’s screening also reviewed 
the country’s categorization which identified the proposed project activities to fall within 
Schedule II categorization (i.e. small-scale projects with impacts that may not be as serious as 
Schedule I projects depending on size, location and other considerations) aligned with IFAD’s 
category B projects. The SECAP indicates that no activities that will qualify above the current 
categorization will be undertaken. To support this, the SECAP provided for an exclusion list of 
activities that will not be considered under the project. 
38. 
Safeguard instrument and information disclosure: The AE has submitted a social, 
environmental and climate assessment procedures (SECAP) review note as the safeguards 
instrument for the project. It has identified the anticipated potential environmental and social 
risks and impacts which are listed against the project’s components. As the project areas are not 
yet completely defined, there is a need to conduct site-specific environmental and social impacts 
assessment (ESIA) or limited level environmental study (LLES), as appropriate, when the areas 
have been defined and finalized. The AE has disclosed the SECAP on its website in English (as 
English is the official language in Belize). 
39. 
Key environmental and social risks and impacts and mitigation measures: The 
SECAP identified and assessed the potential risks and impacts of the project and provided for 
project level mitigation measures through the environmental and social management plan 
(ESMP) which can serve as guidance in the implementation of the project. The ESMP provides

=== PAGE 100 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 100 
 
 
for mitigation, monitoring and reporting measures on its performance including institutional 
and organizational arrangements and indicative budget. It also provides for measures for 
information disclosure including the process for continued consultation and participation of 
affected people, grievance redress mechanism, and is subject to refinements during project 
implementation. A more focused due diligence will be conducted once the specific locations and 
interventions are finalized and will contribute to the development of specific measures that will 
be integrated into the ESMP. 
(a) 
Labor and working conditions and community health, safety and security: Since workers 
are expected to be mobilized in implementing the project components, the project is 
expected to comply with national employment and labor laws and promote safe and 
healthy working conditions. Rehabilitation works will need to be implemented over 
existing rural secondary roads and small bridges which could lead to potential exposure 
of local communities to risks related to traffic, noise and dust exposure, or untoward 
incidents with private security personnel. The project will assess the potential site level 
health, safety and security (HSS) concerns on the affected communities and ensure 
management of such risks in accordance with the SECAP and guided by the AEs 
Guidance Statement 14 on Community Health.  
(b) 
Resource efficiency and pollution prevention: Small-scale irrigation and drainage systems 
(i.e. no single irrigation scheme will be larger than 50 hectares) will include intake 
structures, pumping stations, rain harvesting ponds, main and secondary distribution 
networks, drainage networks and flood protection structures, among others. Local 
hydrological studies will be conducted to ensure that mitigation measures to reduce 
associated impacts will need to be incorporated in the localized ESMP and good practice 
irrigation technologies (e.g. drip irrigation, water harvesting practices including capture 
of run-off where feasible) will be implemented and not adversely affect multiple water 
users. On-farm irrigation/drainage systems for connecting to main or secondary 
networks (e.g. drip, sprinkler, gravity ditches or field open drains) will be provided by 
the project to irrigate the plots. The project will promote the combined use of surface 
and groundwater including recharge activities. 
(c) 
The SECAP has identified pollution resulting from the use of agro-chemicals and 
pesticides as a potential impact of the project since it may indirectly encourage the use 
of agro-chemicals due to increased quality of farm inputs or water availability for 
irrigation. To manage this, the project plans to engage extension agents to assess viable 
options to chemical pesticides (e.g. organic alternatives and bio-pesticides) in line with 
national and international standards. The project also aims to promote Integrated Pest 
Management (IPM) approaches and has provided for developing a pest management 
plan. Notwithstanding, the project should conduct training to project beneficiaries 
during the implementation phase on handling, storage, application and disposal of 
pesticides and agrochemicals in accordance with the Food and Agriculture 
Organization’s International Code of Conduct on the Distribution and Use of Pesticides 
or other good international industry practice (GIIP). 
(d) 
Land Acquisition: The SECAP indicates that the project will not acquire new lands in the 
implementation of the road rehabilitation and agro-processing facilities or any other 
facilities since it is only expected to improve on the existing land used for such existing 
activities/facilities. Hence, no relocation and/or loss of shelter or livelihoods of the 
households or communities are expected at the implementation stage. The project 
provides for the conduct of an environmental and social screening to ensure that 
activities requiring acquisition of land or that could lead to physical and/or economic 
displacement will be excluded from being supported under the project.

=== PAGE 101 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 101 
 
 
(e) 
Biodiversity and Cultural Heritage: By design, the project will not have any intervention 
inside protected areas and in natural habitats and critical areas. The SECAP also 
excludes the implementation of activities in buffer zones that can adversely affect 
biodiversity and local habitats. Since the project’s infrastructural interventions will be 
on existing facilities, the project is also not expected to affect cultural heritage sites and 
will exclude activities in such areas.  
(f) 
Indigenous peoples: While the project does not anticipate adverse impacts on lands and 
resources of recognized or self-identified indigenous peoples, the SECAP incorporates 
an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) that provides for the process for 
ensuring informed consultation and participation in implementing the activities and 
developing an Indigenous Peoples Plan, including free, prior and informed consent 
(FPIC), as needed. The IPPF is also supported by the AEs guidance documents on FPIC 
process. 
40. 
Institutional arrangements including monitoring and evaluation: The Ministry of 
Economic Development (MED) is identified as the lead executing agency that will ensure the 
creation of a project management unit (PMU). The PMU will host the multidisciplinary team of 
specialists (including safeguards specialist) who will take on the day-to-day implementation of 
the project.  The site level environmental assessment of the proposed design solutions will be 
carried out by the Environment Department of the Ministry of Agriculture, Fisheries, Forestry, 
Environment and Sustainable Development (MOA) which will also undertake the operations 
and maintenance of irrigation and drainage systems through the Water Users' Associations. The 
Ministry of Works will undertake the operations and maintenance of the roads over the life of 
the project. Extension agents will be engaged, and farmer-to-farmer training mechanism will 
also be established. A project monitoring and evaluation system which includes monitoring of 
the ESMP is included in the SECAP with broad project parameters that will need to be 
monitored and managed. During project implementation, further elaboration of the ESMP and 
the monitoring parameters at the site level will need to be prepared to include site specific 
measures. 
41. 
Stakeholder engagement: Consultations undertaken with various stakeholders in the 
preparation stage identified the issues raised are presented in the SECAP. The AE has also 
included an approach for stakeholder engagement for which a plan will be developed with 
details of how the AE will ensure that information related to the project will be disclosed and 
stakeholders will be consulted in later stages of the project. Stakeholder engagement will also 
be undertaken during the ESIA process. 
42. 
Grievance redress mechanism: Information on the AE’s institutional-level grievance 
redress mechanism is included in the SECAP with details of the processes that are followed in 
managing complaints and how the mechanism can be accessed by the stakeholders. A project-
level grievance redress mechanism will also be developed and information on how it can be 
availed and the process for lodging a complaint will be shared to various stakeholders 
throughout the project life. 
4.2 
Gender policy 
43. 
The AE has submitted a gender assessment. Therefore, the funding proposal complies 
with the operational guidelines of the GCF gender policy and action plan. The gender assessment 
has outlined the general context of gender issues in Belize. Roles of men and women in the sector 
of intervention have also been included in the gender assessment. Women are actively involved 
in agricultural activities that contribute to food security of households, in addition to participating 
in agricultural value chains though they are underrepresented in small-farmer producer 
organisations, whilst men are more involved in commercial production of agricultural products.

=== PAGE 102 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 102 
 
 
44. 
Consultations conducted by the AE in January 2019 in the project area identified the 
differential needs of men and women in the context of the project. It identified that there are 
differentiated needs for agricultural inputs and equipment, accessing markets and value 
addition to their produce.  
45. 
A gender action plan has also been prepared by the AE. The gender action plan includes 
indicators and targets against each of the project’s activities. The AE is advised to ensure that 
gender-related activities in the action plan are described in more detail. For example, clarity and 
articulation is required of the specific activities that will be undertaken to ensure better 
representation and participation by women in producer organisations as part of output 2. The 
AE is recommended to articulate in the gender action plan how the needs and priorities of men 
and women that were identified during consultations will be addressed. For instance, there is a 
need to articulate how the matching grant fund will be employed to that effect, and the 
incentives that will be used to ensure women get access to it. 
46. 
An indicator with sex disaggregated targets has been included in the logic framework of 
the funding proposal at the fund-level impacts and at the outcome level. Sex-disaggregated 
targets have also been included for some of the activities in the gender action plan. The baseline 
study which will be undertaken at the beginning of the project as indicated in the gender 
assessment should collect information on gender matters that can be used to rationalise targets 
that have been provided and include sex-disaggregated targets where they have not been 
included.  
47. 
Responsibilities for the implementation of the gender action plan are assigned to the 
project management unit. The AE is recommended to ensure that the unit allocates human 
resources with gender expertise for the continuous implementation, monitoring and evaluation 
of the gender action plan. 
48. 
Costs is not included in the gender action plan. The AE is advised to ensure financial 
resources are allocated from the project budget for the implementation of gender-related 
activities. Timelines need to be aligned with the timeframe, in years, of the implementation of 
the project as indicated in the funding proposal.   
49. 
The AE is recommended to provide a revised gender action plan taking into 
consideration the Secretariat’s assessment findings before commencing the implementation of 
the project. 
4.3 
Risks 
50. 
Overall programme assessment (medium risk):  
(a) 
GCF is requested to provide approximately USD 8 million to increase the resilience of 
smallholder farmers. The overall financing structure is balanced, with GCF financing 
proposed with a split of instruments (76 per cent in grants and 24 per cent in the form 
of a loan). The programme is co-financed by the AE with investments targeting 
increased productivity (machinery and facilities), providing an additional USD 8 million 
loan, together with the Government grant contribution (USD 3.2 million). The 
programme will be further supported by at least 15 per cent equity contributions from 
the beneficiaries; 
(b) 
The programme targets smallholder farmers and has to be executed in six years with 
interventions in the value chain (producers’ organizations) and an upgrade of public 
infrastructure necessary for production (roads, drainage and information systems). 
Good coordination by the implementing entity (i.e. MED) and effective governance will 
be crucial for successful implementation; and

=== PAGE 103 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 103 
 
 
(c) 
The grants resources will be dedicated to the part of the project involving costs that 
cannot be supported by matching revenues (technical assistance, training) and will 
cover the incremental costs of climate proofing productive investments to address 
erratic weather patterns caused by climate change. The GCF grants are therefore 
directly targeting additional costs for agricultural and rural development in the country 
brought about by climate change.  
51. 
Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme 
(low risk): 
(a) 
IFAD, the AE, has long and proven experience in policy advisory and financing 
investments in agriculture, and working experience with the Government of Belize; and 
(b) 
The executing entity is MED and the Ministry of Finance (MOF) is the representative of 
the borrower for all financial and legal aspects of the programme. MED has robust 
experience in capital budgeting and national economic planning as well as oversight in 
the implementation of projects and coordinating activities with financial institutions and 
multilateral development banks.  
52. 
Programme specific execution risks (medium risk): 
(a) 
Governance risk (medium): IFAD will have to ensure that MED and other ministries (e.g. 
the National Climate Change Committee and National Climate Change Office under the 
Ministry of Forestry and the Ministry of Rural Development) take ownership of the 
project through the Programme Oversight Committee so that successful execution is 
widely supported. A potential weakness in coordination of the ministries may delay the 
project or offer suboptimal technical advice to farmers; 
(b) 
IFAD will have to ensure that the Project Management Unit (PMU) receives adequate 
support. Although several stakeholders may be involved, IFAD is familiar with such 
arrangements and should be able to replicate it effectively as in other supported 
operations; 
(c) 
Implementation risk (medium): the recipients of interventions are smallholder 
organizations that currently have difficulties in accessing markets or establishing stable 
production. They will be technically advised to execute productive projects and 
incorporate new technologies to enable income stability on their farms. As nine different 
production models are targeted for more than 6,000 rural households with less than 25 
acres of land, the reach of the project will have to be wide and effective to cover 
adequately the knowledge transfer, follow the implementation and assure stability of 
results over time. This sustained effort by the implementing entities has to be assured 
for 15 years to achieve the forecasted incremental net flows of benefits and reflect 
consolidation of the activities and new technologies; 
(d) 
Equity contribution risk (low): the programme relies on technical advisers and 
Monitoring & Evaluation Managers to verify the beneficiary equity contributions of at 
least 15 per cent of the total cost for all the approved subinvestments. Market demand 
for the proposed technologies is expected to be high on average; however, some of the 
poorest beneficiaries might be unwilling or unable to contribute with the required level 
of equity. GCF may consider a covenant to be included in the term sheet to ensure that 
equity contributions are verified before the related GCF funds are disbursed to the 
subinvestments; 
(e) 
Financial viability (medium): the financial model is carried out for nine products for a 
15-year period. The model assumes the adoption of improved technologies in 
production systems for farmers who have applied similar technologies or have seen 
them operating on other farms. The financial viability shows a financial IRR of 15.74 per 
cent and a net present value discounted at 10 per cent of 9.4 million Belize dollars

=== PAGE 104 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 104 
 
 
(BZD). This level of return may be lower as it depends on the programme effectiveness 
to enable a more profitable farm yield quickly (higher income generation). With regard 
to road infrastructure investments, benefits assume effective reduction in 
transportation costs (e.g. fuel, maintenance) and a reduction in damages and losses of 
the products transported; 
(f) 
Economic viability (medium): the project economic viability shows an economic IRR of 
18.97 per cent and a net present value discounted at 6 per cent of BZD 29 million. IRR 
assumes positive externalities that could be better demonstrated (e.g. increased jobs, 
better quality agri-products). The cash flows use shadow prices to eliminate the effect of 
taxes and subsidies, market distortions, unemployment and the exchange rate that may 
be further elaborated and reported as the project progresses; and 
(g) 
Both financial and economic analyses demonstrate a sufficient resilience in case of 
stressed scenarios (e.g. 10 per cent decrease in output prices and 10 per cent increase in 
costs, one-year delay of project implementation). 
53. 
GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk):  
(a) 
In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in 
terms of the result area and single proposal is not material. 
54. 
Compliance (medium risk): 
(a) 
The counterpart contributions element of the project may pose a higher risk of money-
laundering/terrorist financing due to the commingling of funds from unverified sources. 
Credit unions are generally not regulated/supervised for anti-money-
laundering/counter-terrorist financing (AML/CFT) controls. Furthermore, the funding 
proposal does not indicate the application of strict fund sourcing standards and does not 
contain a description of the measures/strategy the AE will adopt to identify and verify 
the source of funding for the counterpart contributions; and 
(b) 
While the proposal does not provide information on how this risk will be addressed, the 
accreditation master agreement (AMA) contains mandatory AML/CFT provisions, to 
which the AE is required to adhere. These include the obligation to apply international 
best practice standards regarding AML/CFT controls throughout the project’s 
implementation. The AE is further required to cascade and ensure the implementation 
of appropriate AML/CFT due diligence and controls in activities conducted by executing 
entities and other subsidiary third parties involved in the project cycle. Therefore, the 
elevated risk derived from the counterpart contributions aspect may in effect be 
mitigated by the AML/CFT obligations of the AE under the AMA. 
55. 
Conclusion (medium risk):  
(a) 
It is recommended that Board consider the above factors in its decision.

4.4 
Fiduciary 
56. 
As the AE, the role of IFAD will be to supervise the project, provide support and resolve 
issues that may arise during implementation. It will ensure that all fiduciary requirements are 
met, and that the programme’s fiduciary risk is kept at a minimum while monitoring the 
adequacy of the financial management arrangements of the PMU. 
57. 
MED, the executing entity, will be designated as lead programme agency with overall 
responsibility for programme implementation. As part of the design, and in accordance with 
IFAD financial management policies and guidelines, a financial management assessment was 
undertaken for the proposed programme’s arrangements within the lead programme agency, 
MED. 
58. 
MED will ensure proper financial management and implementation of the project 
through the creation of a dedicated PMU. The principal mandate of the PMU will be to carry out 
the overall programming and budgeting and to take the lead for overall project implementation, 
including working with service providers, Government ministries and departments, individual 
beneficiaries, producers’ organizations and the municipalities of the project’s target areas.  
59. 
Two USD accounts will be opened in the Central Bank of Belize, to be maintained by 
MOF, into which the IFAD and GCF funds will be deposited. The PMU will submit requests to 
MOF for the transfer of IFAD and GCF funds from the designated accounts to the operating 
account(s). The Government of Belize counterpart contribution will also be deposited to the 
operating account(s) in quarterly tranches. 
60. 
The programme’s external audit will be conducted by a private audit firm according to 
IFAD Guidelines on Project Audits. The PMU will prepare the terms of reference for the auditor 
for review by IFAD on a no objection basis. Procurement for the proposed project would be 
carried out in accordance with IFAD guidelines and the provisions stipulated in the IFAD 
General Conditions for Financing. 
4.5 
Results monitoring and reporting 
61. 
This project focuses on adaptation and spans three result areas – most vulnerable 
people/communities, health/well-being, food and water security and ecosystem/ecosystem 
services. The expected direct and indirect beneficiaries are 30,000 (12,000 females and 18,000 
males) and up to 95,296 (50/50 gender split), respectively.  
62. 
The revised implementation timetable in section C.8 of the funding proposal 
incorporated comments related to elaborating activity description. It will further benefit by 
addressing another comment mentioned previously regarding showing milestones for 
deliverables associated with the output and/or activity (e.g. timing of submission of an 
assessment report regarding: 1.1 develop a climate vulnerability assessment).  
63. 
Regarding the logical framework, the revised funding proposal addressed most of the 
comments and suggestions provided by the Secretariat. It is also noted that the baseline figures 
will be revised at the start of the project when the baseline survey and subsequent studies are 
undertaken. The AE is recommended to further improve the logic framework with the guidance 
provided by the Secretariat during the second-level due diligence.

4.6 
Legal assessment 
64. 
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 
September 2018, and it became effective on 9 November 2018. 
65. 
The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has 
obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project. It 
is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board: (a) the 
Accredited Entity has obtained all its internal approvals; and (b) the Fund has received a 
certificate or legal opinion from the Accredited Entity in form and substance satisfactory to the 
Fund confirming that all final internal approvals by the Accredited Entity have been obtained, 
and that the Accredited Entity has the authority and capacity to implement the project. 
66. 
The proposed project will be implemented in Belize. The GCF has signed a bilateral 
agreement on privileges and immunities with Belize. 
67. 
In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made 
subject to the following conditions:  
(a) 
Delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion within 120 
days of the Board approval confirming that it has obtained all its internal approvals; 
(b) 
Signature of the funded activity agreement in form and substance satisfactory to the 
Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval or the date the Accredited 
Entity has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all its 
internal approvals and has the capacity and authority to implement the project, 
whichever is later; and 
(c) 
Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP101 
Proposal name: 
Resilient Rural Belize (Be-Resilient)  
Accredited entity: 
International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) 
Project/programme size: 
Small 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium 
1. 
According to the funding proposal, the purpose of the planned activities is to increase 
the adaptive capacities of poor and vulnerable smallholder farmers against climate change (CC) 
threats in Belize. The targeted farmers live in 23 communities distributed across five selected 
priority districts (Belize, Cayo, Orange Walk, Stann Greek and Tolado) in Belize. The three main 
pillars of sustainable development – environmental, economic and social – are tackled in 
parallel by the funding proposal to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers which 
ultimately should increase the target group’s capacity to adapt to CC variations and extreme 
weather events. The outcomes of the funding proposal will contribute to the GCF result areas: 
most vulnerable people and communities; health and well-being, food and water security; and 
infrastructure and the built environment. 
2. 
Based on the United Nations categorization, Belize is a small island developing State. 
According to the Poverty Assessment Report 2010,1 rural poverty grew to 55 per cent of the 
total population in 2009, compared with 28 per cent in urban areas. This inevitably has led to a 
decrease in the adaptive capacities of smallholders, most of whom fall within the poverty range 
and, as a consequence, are more vulnerable to CC exposure. 
3. 
The project has two components and several cross-cutting activities belonging to both.  
4. 
Component 1 is mainly for strengthening smallholder participation in select value 
chains (VC) through the promotion of climate-resilient and environmentally sustainable 
production methods, product diversification, and related innovations. Initially, activities 
planned in this component will focus on the development of six vegetables (tomatoes, sweet 
peppers, hot peppers, cabbages, carrots and onions), one fruit (pineapples), and beekeeping 
products (principally honey). These products were selected from over 20 commodities 
identified by the Government of Belize and community stakeholders through a participatory 
process. This component comprises: 
(a) 
Technical support and the physical presence in the project districts of climate-resilient 
agriculture specialists working with extension agents of the Ministry of Agriculture;  
(b) 
Establishment of a competitive matching grant fund (MGF), funded by a GCF loan and 
the Government of Belize, is part of this component. It will be structured to support climate-
resilient production and value-chain development investments. Producer organizations and 
individual members receiving support from technical areas of the project will be eligible for 
funding;  
                                                                
1 Funding proposal, page 14.

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 108 
 
 
(c) 
Increase food security, self-consumption and healthy food choices through the 
implementation of backyard gardens.  
5. 
Component 2 aims to support public climate-resilient infrastructure enhancing 
smallholder farming business/rural enterprise opportunities while serving the largest number 
possible of direct and indirect project participants. It includes: 
(a) 
Providing investments for rehabilitation and provision of climate-proof roads and 
drainage and irrigation schemes;  
(b) 
Establishing a nationwide climate information system;  
(c) 
Formation of Water user associations and providing technical assistance for 
institutional development and management of irrigation and drainage schemes. 
6. 
Both components are supported by cross-cutting activities: capacity-building, research 
and development (R&D), and policy engagement/consultation. 
7. 
The total project cost is USD 20 million, of which USD 8 million will be provided by the 
GCF – USD 6.09 million in the form of a grant and the rest from a senior loan. The senior loan 
will be a sovereign loan for the government, used to fill the MGF.2 
8. 
As it reported in the funding proposal, the expected number of direct beneficiaries of the 
project is 30,000 people (7.8 per cent of the country’s total population), including 12,000 
women and 18,000 men. Some 3,500 rural households in the villages and communities make up 
the priority areas jointly identified by Government of Belize and the International Fund for 
Agricultural Development (IFAD), the accredited entity (AE), for programme implementation. 
Another 2,500 households from non-priority areas will be identified during implementation, 
totalling 6,000 households. According to the country poverty assessment, poor households in 
rural Belize comprise five people or more, which equals a total of 30,000 direct affected project 
participants. Given that 30 per cent of farmers in Belize are women, farming is a family business 
in which women engage in several farming and marketing activities, and women are the main 
group responsible for backyard gardening, around 40 per cent of all project participants will be 
women and 60 per cent will be men. It is assumed that the project will have an indirect impact 
on an additional 23,243 households engaged in farming in Belize. The number of indirect 
beneficiaries is calculated with the assumption that the average household size is 4.1 people per 
household, which leads to 95,296 indirect beneficiaries. 
9. 
The indicators for assessment of the project impacts are as follows: (i) the expected 
number of rural households engaged in climate vulnerability assessments and trained in climate 
resilience practices and strategies; (ii) the number of households with improved agricultural 
productivity and market opportunities; (iii) the number of rural people with strengthened 
resilience (male and female); (iv) the number of households that have increased resilience and 
food security through backyard gardens; (v) an increase in the number of people (male and 
female) with access to agricultural water supply to ensure water supply during the growing 
season; (vi) kilometres of road constructed, rehabilitated or upgraded to all-weather status; the 
number of regulatory innovations and/or mechanisms for climate-responsive planning and 
development; (vii) the number of households reporting adoption of environmentally 
sustainable and climate-friendly technologies or practices; (viii) the number of facilities 
(covered structures, storage facilities) rehabilitated/constructed for CC; and (ix) the number of 
people receiving climate information for decision-making. 
10. 
The impact of activities planned in the funding proposal is assessed as medium at this 
stage because only a pre-feasibility study on CC risks and relevant adaptation measures is 
                                                                
2 The MGF will cover – including a project participant contribution (15 per cent) – investments in sustainable and 
climate-resilient production, such as hurricane-adapted low-cost greenhouses, renewable energy pumps, 
agroforestry systems and technical assistance for climate-resilient practices.

=== PAGE 109 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 109 
 
 
provided in the submission. This will be updated to a feasibility study after the project initiation, 
and some identified CC risks, smallholder farmers’ needs, and relevant measures may be revised 
later. Training materials, which should be based on a final feasibility study, are also not 
provided at this stage of assessment. In addition, the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
(TAP) is concerned that (based on the budget distribution) too much focus is placed on the 
development of training materials and study tours over investment activities. 
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
 
 
Scale: High 
11. 
There are several activities in the project concept that could be considered as 
transformational for increasing the CC resilience of agriculture and rural development in Belize. 
These are: 
(a) 
Consideration of CC-related risks when planning agriculture and rural developments. 
Assisting smallholder farmers in assessment of the CC impact on the value chains and in 
development with business proposals for their MGF application; 
(b) 
Support of loan application activities for financing the adaptation activities through 
MGF; 
(c) 
Introduction to renewable energy technologies in the agriculture sector, such as solar 
panels and solar pumps;  
(d) 
Conducting special study to assess mechanisms which provide Belizean smallholders 
with climate disaster insurance, allowing them to make production investments with 
greater financial security3. This study will be conducted within the cross-cutting 
component of the funding proposal, an applied R&D.  Potential mechanisms include an 
index-based or conventional indemnity insurance scheme (subcomponent 1.2, cross-
cutting activities policy); and 
(e) 
Assessment of feasibility of using ecosystems payment services to stimulate 
maintenance and restoration of local ecologies (e.g. native forests) which provide 
essential environmental services but do not currently generate revenue for smallholder 
farmers and other landowners will be also conducted within the same R&D component. 
12. 
Potential for transformational changes is high, though the independent TAP considers 
that practical implementation of R&D initiatives considered under d) and e) would be more 
appropriate target for GCF. 
1.3 
Sustainable development potential  
 
 
Scale: High 
13. 
The sustainable development potential of the funding proposal is high, contributing to 
six Sustainable Development Goals: 
(a) 
Goal 1: No poverty - “Economic growth must be inclusive to provide sustainable jobs 
and promote equality.” The poorest people and communities in Belize are 
predominantly rural, and their livelihoods depend heavily on climate-sensitive sectors 
such as small-scale agriculture, fishing, and tourism (which impact their food security 
and livelihoods). Moreover, a large proportion of the poor living in coastal areas are 
exposed to the risks of hurricanes and storm surges, with those living near watersheds 
prone to flooding. These areas generally have lower levels of protective infrastructure, 
                                                                
3 A mechanism for smallholder farmers to access a disaster recovery fund for Caribbean countries – launched by the 
Caribbean Development Bank and Caribbean Catastrophe Risk Insurance Facility within the Integrated Sovereign 
Risk Management in the Caribbean project – is not yet defined.

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 110 
 
 
and housing is of lower quality, thus increasing their vulnerability to hurricane and 
flood risks. Rural poverty is dominated by agricultural workers. The project mainly 
targets poor smallholder farmers and will ensure their inclusiveness in the decision-
making process; 
(b) 
Goal 2: Zero hunger – “The food and agriculture sector offers key solutions for 
development, and is central for hunger and poverty eradication.” As described in the 
funding proposal, in general, poverty in Belize is associated with living from hand to 
mouth (and some days experiencing hunger), a lack of jobs, inadequate or absent 
amenities, poor housing, a lack of good-quality water, education and/or enough money 
to send children to school, and a general struggle to survive. This project aims to 
increase the adaptive capacity of poor smallholder farmers and reduce poverty and 
hunger by supporting them to increase the productivity and quality of their agricultural 
products and increase accessibility to market;  
(c) 
Goal 5: Gender equality – “Gender equality is not only a fundamental human right, but a 
necessary foundation for a peaceful, prosperous and sustainable world.” As the Ministry 
of Agriculture is one of the few Government of Belize ministries with a gender focal 
point, the project will support activities designed/led by the focal point team. The 
projects’ strategy for social inclusion and gender is based on the mainstreaming of both 
concepts throughout most activities in order to increase the participation of women and 
young people; 
(d) 
Goal 8: Economic growth – “Decent work and economic growth.” The project is oriented 
around economic growth in rural areas and, in particular, the agriculture sector, aiming 
to increase the resilience of rural infrastructure and agriculture activities to CC and 
economic shocks;  
(e) 
Goal 13: Climate change – “Educate young people on climate change to put them on a 
sustainable path early on.” The project is dedicated to developing climate risk-related 
information and providing training to the different stakeholder groups (e.g. government, 
extension centres/officers, local smallholder farmers, etc.); and 
(f) 
Goal 15: Biodiversity, forests, desertification – “Plant a tree and help protect the 
environment.” This includes improvement of soil management and an increase in the 
resilience of crops and many other activities that contribute to this goal. 
14. 
The sustainable development potential of the funding proposal is high as far as, 
conceptually, the project’s purpose is to change business-as-usual development to sustainable 
development of agriculture sector. 
1.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
 
 
 
Scale: High 
15. 
As a small island developing State in the Caribbean hurricane belt, Belize is highly 
vulnerable to the impacts of CC. While the most extreme and visible effect is the increased 
intensity of tropical storms and hurricanes, CC also manifests itself through increased 
temperatures and sea-level rise as well as the documented trends of increased droughts, 
flooding and significant rainfall pattern variations. Belize is one of the most vulnerable countries 
to CC in the world. An assessment of individual countries’ vulnerability to climate threats, 
prepared by the non-governmental organization Germanwatch, revealed that over the 20-year 
period 1996–2015 Belize was ranked twenty-sixth out of 181 countries in the Global Climate 
Risk Index.4 This research identified important vulnerabilities in the food sector, affecting food 
security, nutrition and livelihoods.  
                                                                
4 Available at <https://germanwatch.org/de/download/16411.pdf>.

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 111 
 
 
16. 
According to a recent systematic country diagnostic by the World Bank Group, Belize is 
within the group of countries most adversely affected by weather-related events and other 
natural hazards. As such, Belize incurs annual losses of close to 4 per cent of gross domestic 
product due to natural disasters (Carneiro, 2016).  
17. 
Several studies, including the country’s second and third national communications state 
that an increase in temperature has an adverse effect on economically important crops to the 
country, such as sugarcane and citrus. Furthermore, higher temperatures resulting from CC 
have already caused soil aridity, a proliferation of pests and diseases and put pressure on water 
resources for water irrigation purposes. Sea-level rise has already intensified flooding and 
saline intrusion leading to soil salinization. 
18. 
The new agricultural policy framework (National Food and Agricultural Policy 2015–
2030) and the National Adaptation Strategy to Address Climate Change in the Agriculture Sector 
(launched in 2015) emphasize the importance of the sector to Belize’s economy and highlight 
the need to implement adaptation measures and increase product value to ensure maintenance 
of food security. Both policy documents also stress the vulnerability of small-scale/family 
farmers and their crucial role in rural development.  
19. 
In particular, the National Adaptation Strategy to Address Climate Change in the 
Agriculture Sector in Belize reports that CC and climate variability will impact agriculture 
systems and practices such as soil fertility and land preparation; pest and disease control; and 
water requirements (excess and deficits). Higher temperatures will cause increased stress on 
current livestock breeds, and crop types and varieties. It is very likely that climate change and 
climate variability will result in reduced rainfall overall, but the most detrimental effect is likely 
to come from the variation in the seasonal distribution of rainfall, leading to more periodic 
droughts and floods. 
20. 
A preliminary survey of different stakeholders (policy makers, agriculture producers 
and support institutions) revealed that pests/diseases and soil nutrition management were 
considered the most critical adaptation needs for Belize.  
21. 
In the regions considered by the funding proposal, the following CC-related threats and 
adaptation needs were identified: 
(a) 
Toledo. This is the district with the highest risk of increased drought frequency and 
severity. Land productivity dynamics revealed that 68,304 hectares (ha) reported 
declining productivity, an additional 11,963 ha presented early signs of decline, and 
29,707 ha are currently stable but stressed. The selected VC, pineapple, is sensitive to 
rainfall variability but highly adaptable with irrigation. There is a high willingness 
among local farmers to adopt different crop varieties that better adapt to droughts; 
(b) 
Stan Creek. This district is at risk of increased drought frequency and severity, and 
hurricanes. Land productivity dynamics revealed that 59,566 ha reported declining 
productivity, an additional 7,202 ha presented early signs of decline and 35,877 ha are 
currently stable but stressed. The selected VC, hot pepper, is highly sensitive to the 
salinity of soil and waters, and water stress. It is better adapted to hot climates. There is 
a high willingness of local farmers to adopt different crop varieties that better adapt to 
droughts; 
(c) 
Cayo. This district reported the greatest decline in productivity and is at risk of an 
increase in flood/drought frequency. Land productivity dynamics revealed that 194,046 
ha reported declining productivity, an additional 5,086 ha presented early signs of 
decline and 75,947 ha are currently stable but stressed. The selected VC, sweet pepper, 
is highly sensitive to salinity of soil and waters, pests, diseases and water stress. Another 
selected VC, cabbage, is irrigation dependant, highly sensitive to water limitation and 
sensitive to flooding, but can benefit from warmer temperatures. The third selected VC, 
tomato, is moderately sensitive to reduced irrigation and salinity and can benefit from

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 112 
 
 
warmer temperatures within a tolerance range. It is sensitive to flooding in high 
temperatures; 
(d) 
Belize. This district is at risk of increased flooding frequency and severity, and most at 
risk from increased frequency/intensity of hurricanes. Land productivity dynamics 
revealed that 101,969 ha reported declining productivity, an additional 7,961ha 
presented early signs of decline and 55,756 ha are currently stable but stressed. The 
selected VC, onion, is irrigation dependent, and sensitive to saline soils, warmer 
temperatures and excessive rainfall. It is adaptable with shading/protective screens, 
controlled irrigation and adequate drainage systems. Other selected VCs are tomato and 
sweet pepper, already described above; and 
(e) 
Orange Walk. This district is at risk of increased flooding frequency and severity, and 
most at risk of increased frequency/intensity of hurricanes. Land productivity dynamics 
revealed 175,655 ha reported declining productivity, an additional 2,872 ha presented 
early signs of decline and 50,711 ha are currently stable but stressed. The vulnerability 
of selected VCs, onion and carrot, are described above.  
22. 
The target group of the project is smallholder farmers with fewer than 25 acres of land, 
who are engaged in part-time or full-time farming or are agricultural workers and have the 
willingness and potential for improving productivity and access to markets. This group can be 
defined as poor, those whose income is below the poverty line, and vulnerable rural families, 
whose incomes are 25 per cent over the poverty line but likely to fall into poverty as a result of 
increased CC effects (e.g. drought, floods) and economic external shocks (changes in prices). 
23. 
Based on the information provided in the national strategies and communications, and 
the adaptation needs of selected regions identified through the pre-feasibility study, the 
independent TAP concludes that the need for further investigation into problems and 
implementation of adaptation needs is high for the country and for selected districts.  
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
 
Scale: High 
24. 
In its nationally determined contribution (NDC), the Government of Belize considers 
adaptation to CC as a high priority. In addition to its vulnerability to natural disasters and 
climate-related shocks, Belize’s small and open economy, geographical location and the lack of 
vital resources constrain its capacity to adapt to the effects of CC and variability in the short and 
long term.  
25. 
The NDC for Belize is consistent with the overall goal of the Growth and Sustainable 
Development Strategy (GSDS) of the country, which encompasses medium-term (2016–2019) 
economic development, poverty reduction, and longer-term sustainable development. The GSDS 
is the nation’s primary planning document and outlines critical success factors for the country’s 
development and to ensure a better quality of life for all Belizeans, living now and in the future. 
This planning document also provides detailed guidance on priorities and on specific actions to 
be taken during the planning period, including actions that contribute to longer-term 
development objectives beyond 2019.  
26. 
The National Climate Resilience Investment Plan (2013) provides the framework for an 
efficient, productive and strategic approach for building economic and social resilience and 
development. Special importance is given to building climate resilience and improving disaster 
risk management capacities across all sectors. 
27. 
The National Climate Change Policy, Strategy and Action Plan, 2015-2020, provides 
policy guidance for the development of an appropriate administrative and legislative 
framework, in harmony with other sectoral policies, for the pursuance of a low-carbon 
development path for Belize.

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 113 
 
 
28. 
In all of the listed documents, agriculture is considered one of the most vulnerable 
sectors of the economy to CC. Adaptation measures considered in the NDC highlight the need to 
improve both crop and livestock husbandry practices; increase access to drought tolerant crops 
and livestock breeds; adopt better soil and water management practices; reduce post-harvest 
losses; and provide early-warning/meteorological forecasts and related information to be 
competitive in the region.  
29. 
Capacity-building efforts around adaptation planning is a priority. In addition, 
resource needs are expected to be significant over the period used to implement the 
adaptation actions that are specified in the national CC action plan. Some specific CC 
adaptation needs in the sector plans include the need to educate different stakeholder 
groups about CC adaptation measures and to help them develop their capacity to research, 
develop and implement adaptation strategies. Some institutions have recognized the need 
to shift from general public awareness and education to a community-based approach for 
environmental education programmes, which would address specific issues and concerns. 
This is key concept of the funding proposal. 
30. 
All the strategies listed above and in other documents, such as the national 
communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change, 
demonstrate a very deep analysis of the CC vulnerability of agriculture and rural 
infrastructure as well as the country’s adaptation needs. The quality and detail of the 
reports are very high, which demonstrates the country’s clear ownership of CC adaptation 
problems. The activities planned in the funding proposal directly contribute to this priority 
area of the country’s GSDS and to the approach to shift from general public awareness and 
education to a community-based approach for environmental education programmes. 
Therefore, country ownership is considered as high. 
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
 
 
Scale: Medium 
31. 
Similar to the impact assessment, the opinion of the independent TAP is that a better 
balance between the investment component and technical assistance (training and consultancy) 
would better fit GCF policy.  
32. 
Through different national documents and a pre-feasibility study provided by the 
accredited entity, it was identified that soil fertility and pests/diseases are the main CC risks for 
the agriculture sector in selected districts considered in the funding proposal. However, the 
funding proposal considers rehabilitation of public roads and irrigation systems as key 
adaptation activities for investment against floods and droughts, and for the increase of the 
adaptive capacity of smallholders. Consequently, it is the opinion of the independent TAP that, 
after a full and inclusive feasibility study is conducted for these districts, some risks and 
adaptation measures might be changed. 
33. 
In addition to the above, the efficiency and effectiveness of the project should be 
assessed based on a feasibility study for adaptation measures and training materials developed. 
The pre-feasibility study and draft outline of training materials submitted at this stage of the 
assessment do not provide enough evidence on which to categorize the efficiency of the 
implemented activities as high. Therefore, these criteria are assessed as medium. 
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
34. 
Taking into consideration the high climate change vulnerability index of the country, 
needs of target groups and urgency of actions, the iTAP recommends approval of this FP subject 
to the following condition:

=== PAGE 114 ===
GCF/B.22/10/Add.03 
Page 114 
 
 
Prior to the first disbursement of funds by GCF under the funded activity agreement, the AE 
shall deliver in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat: 
(a) 
Updated Appendix 11 of the Funding Proposal - Programme Implementation Manual 
including the criteria for the selection of sub-projects (climate change, financial, social, 
environmental) eligible to receive financing by MGF (Matching Grant Fund) and IIFA 
(Infrastructure Investment Funding Award), such eligibility criteria shall take into 
consideration sector/measure specificities and climate change dimension;  
Prior to the second disbursement of funds by GCF under the funded activity agreement, the AE 
shall deliver in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat: 
(a) 
Final outline and the first draft of the training materials, prepared on the basis of the 
feasibility study, to be used during the project implementation, which shall be delivered by the 
Accredited Entity to the Fund before the start of the training activities; 
(b) 
A document containing the description of some sub-projects for each sector (increase 
road resilience, irrigation systems, renewable energy use in irrigation systems, increase crop 
resilience or fertility of soil, etc.), which are considered as potential adaptation measures.

Impact potential 
IFAD acknowledges ITAP’s assessment. Recommendations have been included in FP and will be 
further refined in PIM. The detailed vulnerability/value chain analysis at the regional level will 
be conducted in Year 1 of project implementation with project stakeholders. This participative 
methodology will directly lead to on-site investment to increase smallholders’ resilience to 
climate change. A revised training materials outline is provided as per ITAP recommendation, 
and materials will be fully developed in Year 1 of the project as agreed with ITAP and GCF. 
Project funding is largely targeted towards investment (80% of GCF funding and 64% of project 
total), noting that TA is essential to promote a shift towards climate resilient agriculture.

Part I: Gender Analysis/Assessment: 
Belize has a total population of 395,882 inhabitants according to the Labor Force Survey of April 2018. The rural 
population is slightly higher than the urban population (55% and 45% respectively) and equally slight is the 
difference between the percentage of men and women (56% and 54% respectively). 
Belize has taken important steps in fulfilling its international commitments in favour of gender equality and equity 
and has renewed its Gender Policy in 2013. The government and civil society participation was part of this renewal, 
which generated a consensual document, with important modifications in relation to the previous one. 
Compliance with the Gender Policy requires efforts on the part of different sectors and governmental, non-
governmental and civil society entities, which are still incipient. However, the progress made in central issues such 
as health and education are a sign that it is on the right track. 
The Government of Belize, recognizing that rural women are the most affected by poverty and lack of access to 
services and resources for personal development and for their contribution to the development of their 
communities and territories, participates in the framework of the Agricultural Council Central American (Council of 
Ministers of Agriculture of the Central American Integration System Region and the Dominican Republic) in the 
development of an Agenda for the Economic Empowerment of Rural Women and has a focal point in the Ministry 
of Agriculture and in the Women and Family Support Department (Ministry of Human Development) to implement 
this agenda at the national level and give a better response to these women. Human, material and financial 
resources are currently insufficient and it will be necessary to strengthen the institutional ones in order to 
generate the expected results.

Poverty rate 
Poverty in Belize affects 40% of households and 41.3% of its inhabitants. 13% of 
non-poor households are in a vulnerable situation and 14% of the population is in 
that situation.The sex of the individual has little influence on poverty rates with 
men having slightly higher poverty rates (42% of men are poor compared to 40% of 
women). 
Source: IFAD. 2009. Poverty Assessment Report
